| ADR-016 | 2026-08-22 | **`frontend/scripts/patch-schema-jsonvalue.mjs` runs automatically as the second step of `npm run generate:api`, rewriting two lines of the `openapi-typescript`-generated `src/api/schema.ts`** | `openapi-typescript` 7.13.0 cannot emit a type-checkable definition for the real OpenAPI schema's self-referential `JsonValue-Input`/`JsonValue-Output` (a legitimate recursive "any JSON value" union, `anyOf` containing itself via array items and `additionalProperties`) — TypeScript raises TS2502 on the generated `components["schemas"]["JsonValue-Input"]` self-reference, a checker limitation with indexed-access self-reference, not a shape defect. The three alternatives were all worse: hand-editing `schema.ts` directly violates this prompt's explicit "generated, never hand-written" instruction and the file's own "do not make direct changes" header; suppressing the two lines with `@ts-expect-error` still requires touching the generated file by hand on every regeneration; leaving `tsc --noEmit` red blocks the `build`/CI gate entirely. The patch script re-spells the identical union as two named type aliases (same members, same recursion — only reachable by name instead of by the bracketed index TypeScript can't verify) and fails loudly (a thrown error, not a silent no-op) if the schema's shape ever stops matching what it expects, so a real future change to this schema is never silently mis-patched | — (works around a `tsc`/codegen interaction; does not change any schema semantics) | 1. `src/api/schema.ts` remains 100% generated in the sense that matters — no human ever hand-edits it; the patch script is the last, versioned step of the same `npm run generate:api` pipeline, re-derived from `openapi-typescript`'s own output every run. 2. If a future `openapi-typescript` release fixes this upstream, the patch script's guard regexes will no longer match, it will throw, and removing the now-unneeded step is a one-line `package.json` edit with this ADR as the paper trail. 3. `frontend/README.md` and the script's own header comment both point back to this ADR |
| ADR-017 | 2026-09-02 | **Close D-62 via Option B (`d62-design.md` §4): widen the `sdk.generic.Scheduler` Protocol with `spawn(coro, *, agent_id, virtual_ready_ts_ms=0) -> str` (mirroring `runtime.scheduler.Scheduler.spawn`'s own existing, currently-unused signature at `runtime/scheduler.py:752`) and wire `sdk/langgraph.py::run_node_async` to spawn each instrumented node body as its own scheduler task instead of running as one plain coroutine inside the scheduler's single root task. LangGraph's own Pregel executor keeps deciding what runs each superstep and keeps owning superstep barriers and write application; AgentDX's scheduler now controls *when* each node body's internal execution proceeds relative to other concurrent node bodies and LLM calls, via its existing seeded `_choose` policy — not by driving Pregel's step API (Option A) or by leaving LangGraph unwired (Option C)** | Option A (scheduler drives Pregel superstep-by-superstep) was rejected for reaching into LangGraph's non-public step API — `d62-design.md`'s own highest-risk option, most likely to break on a LangGraph upgrade. Option C (generic path only) was rejected for unblocking none of G1/G4/G9/G10, since all three reference fixtures are LangGraph graphs. Option D (don't declare deadlock while the event loop has pending work) remains recorded as a smaller, ADR-free alternative but is not chosen here: it risks I1 in a way `d62-design.md` §3a itself flags as unresolved, and needs its own experiment first. Option B's own load-bearing assumption — "whether determinism holds depends on Pregel's own ordering being deterministic, which is an assumption to verify, not assume" — was checked before this ADR was written, not after: `tests/integration/sdk/test_pregel_reducer_write_order_is_deterministic.py` (this session) builds a 4-worker fan-out into one `operator.add` reducer channel (`research_fanout`'s own shape), randomizes each worker's real completion delay, and confirms against the project's pinned LangGraph (1.2.10) that `pregel/_algo.py::apply_writes` sorts tasks by path before folding writes into a channel — 80 runs, 23 of 24 possible real completion orderings observed, exactly 1 final write order every time. Reading `apply_writes` directly confirms the mechanism: `tasks = sorted(tasks, key=lambda t: task_path_str(t.path[:3]))` runs before writes are grouped by channel, so a reducer's fold order is a fixed function of graph structure, not of which worker happens to finish first | PRD §3480 names only `Scheduler.run()`/`Scheduler.register(task)` in the scheduler's API table and is silent on `spawn` — this is additive surface, not a contradiction of stated PRD text, so no PRD section is named as overridden | 1. `sdk.generic.Scheduler` becomes a two-method Protocol (`yield_point`, `spawn`); `runtime.scheduler.Scheduler` already satisfies the widened Protocol via its own pre-existing, currently-dead `spawn()` — no new method on the concrete scheduler, only a new method on the abstraction the SDK programs against. 2. This closes D-62's *causal* question and licenses an implementation; it does not itself close D-62 — G3 (real fixtures actually executing) and G2 (zero races on `research_fanout`, now for real rather than a synthetic harness) must both be re-run and must both still pass once Option B is built, per `d62-design.md` §5, before either gate or D-62 itself is marked closed. 3. D-55 (`explore/`'s FR-6 proven only against synthetic Scheduler harnesses, never the literal fixtures) is **not** closed by this decision — Option A would have; this trade is accepted, not overlooked, because D-55 is P1/cut-safe (§5) while G1/G4/G9/G10 are not. 4. The property test above is a regression guard, not a one-shot: if a future LangGraph upgrade ever changes `apply_writes`'s sort key or removes it, this test is what should catch it before Option B's wiring silently stops being safe |
| ADR-019 | 2026-09-03 | **Close D-62 task #25's dispatch gap via candidate β (`d62-design.md` §8.7): `Scheduler.begin_call`'s mint branch flips the calling task's owning identity to `BLOCKED` (recorded in a new `self._backgrounded: set[str]`) when it is currently `RUNNING`; `_drive_coro`'s completion `finally` clears that membership only once the task genuinely reaches `DONE`; `_scheduler_loop`'s deadlock branch grants real ticks up to `resume_drain_ticks` before declaring deadlock whenever `self._backgrounded` is non-empty. `end_call` is unchanged — no re-dispatch through `_resume_task`, ever** | Two prior candidates were tried and rejected first (`d62-design.md` §8.6): a reverted parent-block/*wake* attempt broke completion (`RuntimeError`, re-dispatching an already-consumed coroutine); candidate α (nested dispatch reusing `_choose`/`self._step`) broke `explore/`'s `decision_step` addressing (P13, gates **G2, never-waived**) in two different numbering schemes, both confirmed empirically, because it introduced a second kind of scheduling decision `explore/`'s model has no representation for. β takes only the *working* half of the first attempt (block the parent) and never rebuilds the wake half; dispatching a fanned-out node body still happens through `_scheduler_loop`'s ordinary top-level `_choose()` path, so `self._step` and `explore/`'s addressing are never touched at all — not fixed around, simply never in the blast radius. Validated against all three real fixtures end-to-end (not a synthetic harness): `code_pipeline`, `research_fanout`, `support_triage` all reach `run_end` with `status: complete` for the first time in this project's history, and two independent `code_pipeline` runs at the same seed produce event logs byte-identical across every field except `pid` (a documented volatile field, PRD §10.7) | Same as ADR-017/018: additive surface (`self._backgrounded`, `begin_call`'s block logic, one new deadlock-branch check), no PRD section named as overridden | 1. **Found while validating, not caused by this change, and fixed in the same pass**: a second, pre-existing bug in `analysis/timing.py::resolve_container` — a zero-width `agent_step` span with no children (e.g. `planner`, one `state_write`, nothing nested) had no `TimingNode` for a later-recorded `message_send` (deliberately stamped with the producer's span_id after that span closes, `sdk/langgraph.py::deliver_edges`) to resolve against, raising `TimingAnalysisError`/`E-ANLZ-001`. Confirmed via `git diff` that this file had zero prior changes this session — unreachable before simply because no real fan-out run had ever completed to feed it. Fixed: when `_segment_intervals` reports no gaps *and* the span has no children at all, synthesize its own whole interval as one segment, four lines, scoped to exactly that condition. 2. `tests/integration/cli/test_exit_codes.py::test_exit_5_internal_error_on_a_real_scheduler_deadlock` asserted `code_pipeline`'s fan-out was unreachable by the scheduler — no longer true; converted to the same `monkeypatch`-`_execute_one` substitution the file's other four exit-code tests already use, module docstring corrected. `tests/acceptance/__init__.py` and `test_gates.py`'s G1/G9 docstrings corrected too: G1's blocker is no longer the deadlock but a separate, pre-existing gap found while checking this — `agentdx run` has no `--assert` option at all (PRD §44.1's literal G1 command uses one). Whether G1 itself now passes once `--assert` exists is **not verified** — this sandbox has no Python ≥3.12 and no `agentdx` on `PATH` to run the gate's real subprocess command with, the same standing limitation every acceptance-gate row here carries. 3. Full suite (`tests/api/` excluded from collection, PEP 695 syntax unparseable on this sandbox's Python 3.10.12, same **D-66** gap): 2136 passed, 1 failed, 13 deselected — the one failure is `test_doctor_passes_on_a_healthy_setup`, a true, correct diagnosis by `doctor`'s own `python-version` check of this sandbox's real interpreter being outside the pinned `>=3.12,<3.13` range, confirmed unrelated to this pass via `git diff` (neither the test nor `doctor.py` was touched) — the exact pre-spike baseline otherwise, with the one prior scheduler-related failure now passing rather than reverted away. `ruff`/`mypy --strict`/`check_ledger.py`/`check_bench_markers.py` all clean. 4. **D-62 task #25 is now fully closed**: identity (ADR-018) and dispatch (this ADR) were the two separable questions it split into; both are closed, and all three reference fixtures complete end-to-end under the real `Scheduler` for the first time. 5. **Not done**: no independent OP-2 of the β mechanism, the `_backgrounded`/deadlock-grace-period reasoning, or the `resolve_container` fix; no decisive test promoted into `tests/` for β specifically (ADR-018 got one — `test_d62_fanout_dispatch.py` — this ADR's own validation is real CLI runs read back from SQLite, not yet a tracked test); G1's real pass/fail status once `--assert` exists is owed to the owner on real hardware |
| ADR-020 | 2026-09-03 | **Implement D-80/C-34's ruling for real (`d78-plan.md`, `run_id` collision on re-run): `cli.host.CliRunHost.open_run` checks `store.get_run(run_id)` before `create_run`. Sealed → raise `RunAlreadyExistsError(record)` for `cli.commands.run` to catch, re-score from the stored log via a new `_score_reused`, and exit with the reused verdict's own code (never a blanket 0). Unsealed → `Store.discard_orphan_run(run_id)`, then proceed exactly as a fresh run** | D-80 (2026-09-01) ruled the split (reuse-and-print a sealed collision, replace an unsealed one) but left it, in its own words, "implementation owed" — G9 (never-waived, I7) stayed blocked on this alone once ADR-019 closed the dispatch gap in front of it (owner's own real-hardware `just acceptance` run, same date, surfaced this directly: `code_pipeline` finally reached `E-STORE-010` for the first time). `d78-plan.md` §5.1's own text says an unsealed orphan's row and events should be deleted — this ADR is that plan's own execution, not a new decision | PRD is silent on re-run semantics at all (D-78's own framing); D-80 already settled what "correct" means, this ADR is the implementation, not a second ruling | 1. **A real conflict the plan itself did not spell out, found while implementing rather than guessed around**: `events_no_delete`/`events_no_update` (`store/migrations/m0001_initial.py`) are *unconditional* — `BEFORE DELETE ON events ... RAISE(ABORT)`, no exception for an unsealed row — so §5.1's "delete the orphan's events" cannot be done with a plain `DELETE`. Found by reading the trigger DDL directly, not assumed. **Stopped and asked the owner rather than picking a workaround unilaterally** (two real options: narrowly bypass the trigger inside one guarded transaction, modeled on `store.migrations._apply`'s own identical precedent for a `rewrites_events=True` migration; or a bigger redesign tracking per-attempt ranges so orphaned events are never deleted at all, only ignored on read) — owner picked the narrow bypass. **Built as `Store.discard_orphan_run`**: re-reads `sealed_at` inside the same transaction that drops the triggers (closes the race a caller could otherwise hit between check and delete), drops `events_no_delete`/`events_no_update` via the same public `trigger_names()` the migration runner itself uses, deletes just that `run_id`'s rows from `events` and `runs`, reinstates the triggers from `MIGRATIONS[0].triggers` (no second copy of the DDL), and refuses to return (`E-STORE-006`) if either trigger is somehow still missing afterward — the same defensive shape `store.migrations._require_triggers` already uses for the identical situation. Refuses outright (`E-STORE-005`) on a sealed run; unknown run is `E-STORE-004`. 2. **A second gap found while implementing §4's exit-code mapping, not previously flagged**: reconstructing a sealed run's own verdict from its stored log (`_score_reused`) can recompute `findings`/assertions fully (`analyze_events` needs only the persisted events, I1 guarantees they reproduce a fresh run's own), but **cannot** recompute a `python`-type `success_check`'s result — `run_result.output` (the graph's final return value) is never part of the persisted event log (`RUN_END`'s own payload is counts/timings only, `host.py::close_run`). Not silently faked: `success_check_passed` stays `None` for a reused run, which `evaluate_assertion` already treats identically to "no `success_check` configured at all" — the same value a scenario with none set produces on a fresh run, not a new special case, and any assertion that depends on it correctly reports `not_measurable` rather than a fabricated pass or fail. `faults_fired` **is** fully recoverable this way (a straight count of the run's own persisted `fault_injected` events) and is recomputed rather than defaulted to 0. 3. **`d78-plan.md` §6's own explicit "needs an ADR, do not guess" question — whether `--ci`/JSON marks a reused run distinguishably — resolved here, additively, rather than deferred a second time**: `cli.ci.ScenarioOutcome` gains `reused: bool = False`, same precedent as that dataclass's own `metrics` field ("not one of PRD §22.4's own named top-level keys... load-bearing" comment, reused verbatim in spirit) — omitted from the JSON entirely when `False`, so every consumer that predates D-80 parses an unchanged payload; present and `true` only for a reused run. Human output gets one line: `(reused: identical seed/scenario/graph already ran; not re-executed — D-80)`. 4. **Tests, all five of `d78-plan.md` §7's**: `tests/unit/store/test_discard_orphan_run.py` (5 tests — deletes an orphan's row+events; refuses and changes nothing for a sealed run, §7 test 4; refuses an unknown run; the reinstated triggers still genuinely abort a real UPDATE/DELETE against an unrelated run afterward, not just present by name; the same `run_id` writes cleanly after a discard, §7 tests 3/5's store half) and `tests/integration/cli/test_run_reuse.py` (4 tests, real `code_pipeline` through the real `Scheduler` and CliRunner, no mocks — a passing sealed rerun exits 0 and reuses; a **failing** sealed rerun exits 1, not 0, §7 test 2, the one this whole change most needs to not regress; an unsealed row — `sealed_at` flipped to NULL directly, the same state D-62's own deadlock used to leave, reached without reproducing a live deadlock since `open_run` only ever consults `sealed_at` — is replaced and a real run proceeds, not reused-as-nothing, §7 test 3; `--ci` JSON marks the second invocation `reused: true` and the first not at all). All 9 new tests pass; full suite (`tests/api/` excluded, D-66) 2145 passed / 1 failed (D-66's own `test_doctor`, unrelated) / 13 deselected — 9 more passing than ADR-019's own count, zero regressions. `ruff`/`ruff format --check`/`mypy --strict`/`check_ledger.py`/`check_bench_markers.py` all clean; `check_determinism_hygiene.py` clean on every file this ADR touched (only the pre-existing, unrelated `api/models.py` D-66 gap remains). `lint-imports` could not run in this sandbox at all (package not installed here, a sandbox gap distinct from D-66) — no new cross-layer import was added (`store/sqlite.py` only gained imports from its own sibling `store/migrations/`, already an existing dependency direction), but real confirmation is owed. 5. **G9 (never-waived, I7) is not yet re-confirmed** — this closes D-80's own "implementation owed" gap and the dispatch-gap ADR-019 already closed, but whether `just demo-offline`/`just acceptance`'s G9 now actually passes on real hardware has not been checked; asked the owner to re-run |
| ADR-021 | 2026-09-03 | **`agentdx.run()` (`sdk/generic.py`) calls `await context.scheduler.yield_point("sdk_run_entry")` once, unconditionally, immediately after `open_run()` returns and before `_invoke(graph, payload)` is ever reached** — giving root's own first dispatch one guaranteed scheduler-recognised checkpoint before it ever enters LangGraph's own Pregel/`ainvoke()` entry machinery, so that machinery's own multi-tick settling happens on a *resumption* (which already tolerates it, `resume_drain_ticks`) rather than on the untouched, deliberately-strict first dispatch | Owner re-ran the D-80/ADR-020 verification command on real Python 3.12 hardware and hit a **new** `DeadlockError` (`E-SCHED-003`, empty `wait_reason`, only the root task listed) on the very first invocation of a scenario-file-driven `code_pipeline` run with a real task file (`fixtures/tasks/refactor_module.md`) — before candidate β's own fan-out dispatch gap (ADR-019) was ever reached. Isolated via a direct-target repro (`agentdx run fixtures/code_pipeline --task fixtures/tasks/refactor_module.md --seed 42`, no scenario YAML) that deadlocked identically, ruling out the scenario-file wrapper, and a `RuntimeWarning: coroutine 'LangGraphAdapter._run_node_body' was never awaited` — root's own first `spawn()` call, for the graph's first node, was never reached at all. Traced by reading code, not guessed: `open_run` (this session's own D-80 code included) has zero `await` statements, so it cannot be the stall; `join()` always sets a non-empty `wait_reason` before it suspends, so root never reached that either — meaning root's own coroutine stalled somewhere inside LangGraph's own Pregel/`ainvoke()` entry machinery, the same class of `@shielded` LangChain callback overhead `_resume_task`'s own docstring already documents as needing several real ticks to settle — but only for a *resumption*, never a first dispatch, and root's own first dispatch had never been given that exception. Never exercised by ADR-019's own validation, which ran exclusively via direct fixture targets, always on this sandbox's Python 3.10 (5/5 reruns of the exact failing scenario passed here, not reproduced). **A first, blanket fix was tried and rejected**: granting root's *entire* first dispatch the same `resume_drain_ticks` grace a resumption gets was confirmed, on the owner's own hardware, to fix the real repro — but before being proposed as final, was found in this sandbox to silently break `tests/integration/runtime/test_d62_suspension_contract.py::test_a_suspension_needing_another_task_deadlocks`: with extra real ticks available, that decisive control's own raw, unmanaged `asyncio.Event`/`ensure_future` suspension gets enough ticks to just resolve on its own, defeating the exact non-determinism guard D-62's whole suspension contract exists to enforce — a fix that passes the real repro by breaking that control is not a fix. Per this session's own standing STOP CONDITIONS, presented both the finding and the rejected fix to the owner before writing the real one, rather than resolving unilaterally; owner approved formalizing a narrower fix | PRD is silent on `agentdx.run()`'s own internal dispatch sequencing; `yield_point` already exists on the `Scheduler` Protocol (ADR-017) — this is one additional, unconditional call site, not new surface and not a contradiction of any stated PRD text | 1. Every run now emits exactly one additional `schedule_decision` event at its very start (`reason: "sdk_run_entry"`) — unconditional and content-independent, so I1 is unaffected: every replay of the same seed/scenario/graph takes it identically. Not checked against a real regenerated golden fixture in this sandbox (no Python ≥3.12, standing **D-66**) — owed to the owner's next real-hardware `just ci`/golden-fixture run. 2. **Decisive regression test, both directions**: `tests/integration/runtime/test_root_entry_drain.py` — one test proves root now reaches and returns from its own first `spawn()`/`join()` after a synthetic multi-tick "needs another real task to run" entry ceremony (the same shape as the real bug); a second proves the identical shape, driven directly (bypassing `agentdx.run()`), still deadlocks — the pairing that makes the first test decisive rather than vacuous. Confirmed empirically both ways in this sandbox: manually reverting the `sdk/generic.py` line reproduces `E-SCHED-003` with the identical empty-`wait_reason`/root-only signature the real bug showed; restoring it passes again. 3. Full suite in this sandbox (`tests/api/` excluded, D-66): 2148 passed / 1 failed (D-66's own `test_doctor`, unrelated, unchanged) / 13 deselected — 2 more than ADR-020's own count (the two new tests here), zero regressions. `test_d62_suspension_contract.py`'s own decisive control reconfirmed still correctly raising `DeadlockError` — the exact property the rejected blanket fix would have silently broken. `ruff`/`ruff format --check` clean on both touched files; `mypy --strict` clean on `sdk/generic.py`. 4. **Not yet re-confirmed on real hardware in this final, narrow form** — the owner's own Python 3.12 machine confirmed only the earlier, rejected *blanket* diagnostic against the real repro (both the scenario-file and direct-target forms of it); the narrow, committed version above is behaviourally equivalent for that exact repro (root still gets a resumption's worth of drain grace before its own `_invoke`, just earned through `yield_point` rather than an unconditional grant) but has not itself been re-run there yet. Asked the owner to re-run the same repro command. 5. Candidate β (ADR-019)'s own fan-out dispatch-gap fix is unaffected and untouched by this ADR — this closes a different, earlier-in-the-sequence gap (root's own entry into the graph, before any node — let alone a fan-out superstep — is ever reached), invisible until ADR-019/ADR-020 together let a real run get far enough to expose it for the first time |
| ADR-018 | 2026-09-03 | **Widen `sdk.generic.Scheduler` a second time: `begin_call(*, agent_id: str) -> str` / `end_call(call_id: str) -> None`, bracketing `run_node_async`'s existing `spawn`/`join` call. `begin_call` mints a fresh, private scheduler identity for the call **only** when the real `asyncio.Task` currently executing is not already the recorded owner of the ambient identity it inherited — returning `""` (a no-op, changing nothing) otherwise** | A 2026-09-03 staff-engineer-style audit (`CONTEXT.md` §13, this row) of task #25 — real fixtures deadlocking under fan-out — found `join`'s ambient caller-identity resolution (`runtime.context.current_task()`) collides the moment LangGraph's own Pregel executor dispatches two or more ready nodes concurrently in one superstep: each hidden `asyncio.Task` Pregel creates (`PregelRunner.atick`'s `self.submit()` path, never `spawn`ed by this scheduler) inherits the *same* `SchedTaskContext` its parent had ambient, by ordinary `contextvars` copy-on-`Task`-creation, so concurrent `join` calls silently overwrote each other's `self._task_futures` entry, permanently orphaning one continuation. The audit classified the root cause as **(c)** — a genuine gap in `d62-design.md` §4/§5 (which names the general risk, "two schedulers coexist... reasoned about carefully", but never resolves it into a concrete rule), silently filled by code reusing machinery built for the scheduler's own strictly-sequential dispatch. Per the audit's own STOP CONDITIONS, this was not resolved unilaterally: three repair candidates were laid out, the owner picked "mint a fresh identity, only when needed" over reusing `_task_futures` as a list or keying by `asyncio.current_task()` directly, both of which still needed the same owner-comparison logic once a `Task.state`-sharing race was found underneath them. **The unconditional-mint first draft was wrong, found empirically before commit**: it broke the sequential case `resume_drain_ticks` already fixed, by rebinding the ambient identity away from an already-tracked task's own inline continuation (e.g. Pregel's single-node fast path), silently orphaning that task's own `Task.state` at `RUNNING` forever. `self._identity_owners` (recorded once, by `_drive_coro`, for whichever real `asyncio.Task` legitimately drives each identity) is what let `begin_call` tell "inline continuation of an already-owned identity" from "a different real task that merely inherited a copy of one" | PRD is silent on `spawn`/`join`'s own existence (ADR-017 point 3); this is the same additive surface, one level further, not a contradiction of stated PRD text | 1. Validated two ways: the full existing suite is unchanged (2131 passed, 0 failed, purely additive — nothing but the one new `run_node_async` call site exercises `begin_call`/`end_call`), and an empirical probe built to mirror Pregel's own two dispatch paths directly against the real `Scheduler` (not `ImmediateScheduler`) confirms both the no-op path (sequential) and the fresh-mint path (fan-out) resolve correctly, with no identity collision. 2. **Closes the identity collision. Does not close D-62 or task #25**: a fan-out superstep's spawned node-body tasks still do not get dispatched at all under this fix alone — `_scheduler_loop` cannot reach a fresh `_collect_runnable` while the parent (e.g. root) is mid-resumption, stuck on Pregel's own raw `asyncio.gather`/`asyncio.wait`, which never touches the parent's own `Task.state`. A same-day follow-up (blocking the parent while fan-out calls are outstanding) fixed dispatch — confirmed by trace: both node bodies ran, joined, returned real results, root's own `gather` resolved — but broke on the other half (waking the parent back up double-dispatches an already-consumed coroutine, `RuntimeError`), and was reverted rather than shipped broken. 3. `d62-design.md` §8 records the full trace of both dispatch findings and three unchosen candidate fixes for `_scheduler_loop` itself (teach the drain loop to dispatch other runnable tasks; give the deadlock check a bounded grace period for backgrounded work, done correctly this time; revisit Option A given this changes its cost picture) — none chosen, per owner instruction to pause here for a design review rather than keep iterating unreviewed. 4. All three reference fixtures remain blocked end-to-end pending that decision |
| ADR-022 | 2026-09-03 | **`Scheduler._backgrounded` membership (candidate β, ADR-019) is cleared via a new `_clear_backgrounded_checkin(task_id)` helper, called from the top of `join`/`sleep`/`yield_point` for whichever identity is calling them — not only, as originally built, by `_drive_coro`'s completion `finally` once the task reaches `DONE`. `begin_call`/`end_call` are completely unchanged** | An independent OP-2 audit of candidate β (task #11) found one real, confirmed defect: DONE-only clearing left a stale `_backgrounded` entry for a parent's entire remaining execution once any fan-out call minted under it, well past that specific call's own closing — every real fixture's `tester` stage runs inside exactly this stale window. Consequence: a genuinely unrelated deadlock arising later in that window got an unwarranted ~200-tick `resume_drain_ticks` grace delay it should never have received. **A first fix (reference-counting: `self._call_parent: dict[str,str]`, `self._backgrounded: dict[str,int]`, decremented in `end_call`) was tried and rejected** — it broke 3 existing tests with a *new* `DeadlockError`, root-caused via a trace script to a residual-settling gap: `end_call` runs on the child's own frame the instant its own call closes, but the parent's raw `await` (e.g. `asyncio.gather`) still needs a few more real ticks after that before it actually resolves, and clearing at ref-count-zero removed `_backgrounded`'s grace before that settling finished — the old DONE-only design's staleness was, by accident, also what supplied that grace. Reverted in full before building the real fix, per this session's own standing STOP CONDITIONS discipline (a bug found in one's own first attempt, not a design choice needing owner sign-off, so no gate was raised this time — consistent with owner instruction "let's not wait, move ahead"). The actual fix — clearing on the task's own next check-in rather than on the child's close — costs nothing (identical settling grace, for exactly as long as it is needed) while removing the staleness, since the entry cannot survive past the parent's own next scheduler-visible action | Same additive class as ADR-019/ADR-021: one new private method, three call sites, no schema or PRD text touched | 1. **Two new decisive tests** (`tests/integration/runtime/test_backgrounded_staleness.py`): one reproduces the exact audit scenario (`planner` seq -> `{coder, reviewer}` fan-out -> `tester` seq) and snapshots `_backgrounded` from inside `tester`'s own spawned body — asserts root is already cleared there, well before root's own `DONE`; confirmed genuinely decisive by reverting to the pre-fix code and watching it fail with precisely the predicted stale entry, then restoring the fix. A second, flip-side control snapshots mid-`coder`, before either fan-out call closes, and asserts root is *still* backgrounded — guards against a "fix" that stops populating the dict at all, which would silently reopen the original D-62 task #25 dispatch gap ADR-019 closed. 2. **Verified**: both new tests pass; the exact suite the first, rejected attempt broke (`test_d62_fanout_dispatch.py`, `test_d62_suspension_contract.py`, `test_scheduler.py`, `test_root_entry_drain.py`) is 32/32 with the real fix; full suite (`tests/api` excluded, D-66) clean — only the one pre-existing `test_doctor` failure, zero regressions; `ruff`/`ruff format --check`/`mypy --strict` clean on both touched files. 3. Fixed the audit's cosmetic finding #7 in the same pass: `d62-design.md` §8.7 described `_backgrounded` as `set[str]`; corrected to `dict[str, None]`, and its DONE-only clearing description marked superseded with a pointer to this ADR. 4. **Closes task #11** (independent OP-2 of candidate β) for real — its one confirmed finding is fixed and tested; the audit's other checked-and-cleared hypotheses (multi-way/nested fan-out, exception-during-fan-out, the `resolve_container` fix) are not re-litigated here. 5. **Not independently re-audited** — self-verified only, same standing caveat as every fix this session has shipped; whether every scheduler-call site that should check in actually does, and whether some call shape relies solely on the `_drive_coro`-on-DONE fallback without this session's tests exercising it, is a real open question for a future audit. 6. Not yet re-confirmed on real Python 3.12 hardware — this sandbox's standing D-66 gap |
| ADR-023 | 2026-09-05 | **`agentdx.scenario` is added to `api/`'s permitted-imports list (CONTEXT.md §4) and `.importlinter`'s `api-never-imports-runtime` contract is annotated to say so explicitly** — ratifying, not changing, what the code already did | An independent OP-2 audit of `api/` (2026-09-05, P14, `op2-audit-p14.md` finding #4a) flagged `routes/runs.py`'s `from agentdx.scenario import loader, validate` and `routes/scenarios.py`'s equivalent as undeclared against PRD §24.3/CONTEXT.md §4's layer table, which listed `api/`'s permitted imports as `store, analysis` only. On inspection the imports were never a layering violation in substance: `scenario/` may import nothing (`scenario-is-declarative`, unaffected by this ADR), and the imports are real and load-bearing — `POST /api/runs`'s own scenario resolution and I12 chaos-authorization itself (`inject_fault`) both need to parse and resolve a scenario document, which only `scenario/loader`/`scenario/validate` can do; duplicating that parsing logic inside `api/` would recreate the exact duplicated-source-of-truth defect this project rules against repeatedly (D-12, D-39, D-41, and ADR-013's own precedent for the identical situation in `runtime/`). The gap was that `.importlinter`'s `forbidden_modules` list for `api-never-imports-runtime` never listed `scenario` as forbidden (it never was), but the contract's own explanatory comment never named it as permitted either — true but undeclared until now | PRD §24.3 / CONTEXT.md §4's layer table (`api/` permitted imports — additive: `scenario` joins `store`, `analysis`) | 1. `.importlinter`'s `api-never-imports-runtime` contract gains an explanatory comment naming this ADR; `forbidden_modules` is unchanged (it never listed `scenario`, so no code or config behaviour changes). 2. CONTEXT.md §4's layer table row for `api/` names `scenario` explicitly, mirroring row 129's own `runtime/`→`scenario` precedent (ADR-013) — same shape, one layer over. 3. `scenario/` remains forbidden from importing `api/` in either direction — the dependency stays one-way and acyclic |
| ADR-024 | 2026-09-05 | **`python-multipart` (installed and used since P14) is formally ratified as a CONTEXT.md §3/PRD §24.6-permitted dependency; `pyproject.toml`'s comment beside it, which had cited the wrong ADR, is corrected to cite this one. `ApiConfig`/`agentdx.toml`'s `[api]` table is ruled to need no ADR of its own** | Same audit (`op2-audit-p14.md` finding #4b) found `pyproject.toml`'s `python-multipart` comment cited "ADR-014" as its ratifying authority — a real ADR entry in this ledger, but about an unrelated determinism-exception clause (`AbortGuardMonitor`'s wall-clock read), not any dependency. `python-multipart` is FastAPI's own multipart/form parser (Starlette calls into it for `UploadFile`), required by `POST /api/import`, which PRD §26.1 specifies as a multipart upload — genuinely necessary, already shipped since P14 with no incident, but never recorded under its own ADR number, only mis-cited under someone else's. The audit also raised, without a firm recommendation, whether `ApiConfig`/`[api]` needs its own ADR; ruled here rather than left open a second time: no — `ApiConfig` is not a new *dependency* (AGENTS.md §2's literal scope: "the permitted set is CONTEXT.md §3 plus PRD §24.6 and PRD §25... before it enters `pyproject.toml` or `package.json`") — it is ordinary implementation of already-cited PRD §26 surface, the same class as every other module's own `config.py` section (`SchedulerConfig`, `StoreConfig`, etc.), none of which carries its own ADR | PRD §24.6 (names the API stack's capabilities; `python-multipart` is the concrete distribution FastAPI/Starlette itself requires to implement one of them — `UploadFile`/multipart parsing — the same "capability named, distribution enumerated by ADR" pattern ADR-004 already established for `uvicorn`/`pydantic`/`pyyaml`/`httpx`) | 1. `pyproject.toml`'s comment beside `python-multipart` now cites this ADR, not ADR-014, and explains what actually happened. 2. `ApiConfig`/`[api]` needs no ADR of its own, now or for any future `[api]` field that stays within PRD §26's already-cited scope — this ruling closes that question rather than leaving it for a future audit to re-ask. 3. No `pyproject.toml` behaviour changes — `python-multipart>=0.0.12,<1.0` was already there and already correct; only its citation and this ledger's record of the decision change |

| D-56 | 2026-08-19 | §15.2 (`Schedule = list[(sched_step, chosen_index)]`, `DelaySchedule = dict[sched_step, delay_count]`) | The PRD's own type definitions key a `DelaySchedule` on `sched_step` — the same field a `schedule_decision` event is stamped with | `explore.schedule.DelaySchedule` is keyed on `Turn.decision_step` (`= sched_step - 1`), never `sched_step` itself; `generate.py`'s BFS constructs every child via `{**delay_schedule, turn.decision_step: alt}` | Found empirically during the P13 build, not by reading the PRD twice: `runtime.scheduler.Scheduler._choose()` reads `self._step` *before* it is incremented, but `_scheduler_loop` increments `self._step` immediately after and only then stamps the resulting `schedule_decision` event with the now-incremented value — so the turn recorded with `sched_step=N` was actually decided one step earlier than its own recorded field says. A `DelaySchedule` literally keyed on `sched_step`, exactly as PRD §15.2 defines it, silently steers the *wrong* (next) decision point on a live `Scheduler` every time, with no error raised — proven directly (`tests/unit/explore/test_schedule.py::test_decision_step_matches_live_scheduler`, `..._at_every_branch_point`), not merely reasoned about. The PRD's pseudocode is abstract and does not model a concrete scheduler's pre/post-increment step counter, so this gap could not have been caught by reading §15.2 alone. **Found by a same-session self-audit (2026-08-19) reviewing P13's own diff — this deviation existed in the shipped code from the first commit but was previously undeclared, documented only in code comments, `docs/exploration.md` and the commit message, never as a decision of record. Closed same day, no code change required — only this ledger entry** | Not reconciled against the PRD text — propose amending §15.2 to define `DelaySchedule`'s key as "the scheduler's own decision-point counter at the moment of choice" rather than the literal `sched_step` field name, since the two are not interchangeable against any concrete `Scheduler` implementation that stamps events after advancing its step counter |
| D-57 | 2026-08-19 | §15.3 (`return Report(..., capped=(len(results) >= N), ...)`) | The PRD's own pseudocode computes `capped` as a flat comparison: true whenever exactly `N` results were produced, regardless of whether the frontier still had unexplored work | `explore.generate.explore()` computes `capped=len(results) >= schedule_cap_n and bool(frontier)` — additionally requiring the frontier be non-empty at loop exit | An earlier version of `generate.Budget.exceeded()` folded the schedule-cap and wall-clock checks into one boolean, which `explore()` used directly for `ExplorationResult.budget_exceeded` — so reaching the cap alone also set `budget_exceeded=True`, and `report.format_report` would print "the time budget was exhausted" on a run nowhere near its deadline (a real bug, caught by a hand-computed unit test that failed against the original code). Fixing that bug required checking the cap and the clock as two independent conditions, and the same fix naturally raised a second question the PRD's own literal formula doesn't address: is a run that happens to complete its *entire* frontier at exactly `N` schedules "capped" (implying truncation) or simply complete? The PRD's flat formula says the former; this build's `bool(frontier)` refinement says the latter, on the grounds that "capped" should mean "truncated," not "coincidentally equal to a number." **Found by the same same-session self-audit as D-56 — also previously undeclared, also requiring only this ledger entry, no code change** | Not reconciled against the PRD text — propose amending §15.3's pseudocode to `capped=(len(results) >= N and len(frontier) > 0)`, matching what this build actually ships and what `report.format_report`'s own "reached before the frontier was empty" NOTE text already implies |

The boundary actually observed: the scheduler tolerates `await`, but not an `await` whose resolution requires the event loop to run *another task* — `_collect_runnable` (`runtime/scheduler.py`) only re-examines tasks in `PENDING`/`RUNNABLE`, and a task suspended on anything but a scheduler-created Future (`yield_point`/`sleep`) stays `RUNNING` forever, regardless of whether the real event loop could eventually have resolved it. Fan-out is incidental, not causal. Two findings judged material enough to record here rather than only in the audit file: **(G3-1, MEDIUM)** `tests/acceptance/test_gates.py`'s mechanised G3 gate sets `min_pytest_passed=1` against a 3-test file (`test_replay_equality.py`'s three DoD clauses: 100/100 same-seed replay, same-seed decision-sequence match, different-seed divergence) — a live regression in 2 of the 3 while the first kept passing would still report the mechanised gate PASS. Same discipline additionally applied to **G5**, which previously had no `min_pytest_passed` floor at all (a gap this audit's own numbered findings didn't name, found while implementing the G3 fix and checking for the analogous pattern) — now `min_pytest_passed=4`, matching `test_decomposition_invariant.py`'s confirmed 4-item collection count. **Not repaired, deliberately:** G3-2 (cross-referencing the stronger real-fixture replay-determinism result from the G3 row) and G5-3 (regenerating the golden fixtures against the real scheduler) — both remain exactly as this row originally described, G5-3 explicitly out of proportion for a floor-strictness fix per D-89's own reasoning. **Addendum (2026-09-08): both fixes live-verified.** The owner ran `just acceptance` in full on their own machine: **G3 PASS, G5 PASS** (both with the new stricter floors), **8/10 gates overall**. The other two failures are real and newly surfaced by this same run, not caused by the G3/G5 fix: **G8 FAIL** — a genuine axe-core "nested-interactive" WCAG violation, `Findings.tsx`'s row is a real `<button data-testid="finding-row">` that contains `EvidenceLink.tsx`'s real `<button>` seq/span chips as DOM descendants (mitigated only by `stopPropagation()`, which does not satisfy the nesting rule) — pre-existing since P15/P16, only now caught because P16 Finding #5's axe-coverage-gap repair (already closed, see §13) extended `axe.spec.ts` to this exact route/state for the first time. **G10 FAIL** — timed out at 200s with zero stdout, a third distinct outcome this session (fail outright / clean 67s pass / this 200s+ timeout), run immediately after G8's Playwright/npm e2e suite; resource contention from the prior test is a plausible but unconfirmed cause. **Addendum, same day: G8's nested-interactive violation repaired.** `Findings.tsx`'s `FindingRow` restructured so the row-select `<button>` and `EvidenceLink`'s seq/span `<button>`s are DOM siblings, not ancestor/descendant — the button now wraps only non-interactive content (classification, title, agents, recommendation) and `.rowEvidence` moved out to sit alongside it, in the same place `ReproCommand` already sat as a sibling (an existing pattern in this file, not a new one). The interaction itself is unchanged (row click selects the finding, chip click jumps to that event) — only the DOM shape changed, so no UX/design decision was needed here the way D-86/D-87 genuinely did. `Findings.module.css` updated to keep the visual card seamless (`.row`'s border-radius now top-only, `.rowEvidence` gained matching bottom-rounded border/background, `.rowSuppressed` — which has no evidence sibling — restored to full radius). **Live-verified same day:** the owner ran `npm run test:e2e` — **26/26 passed** (was 25/26; the one prior failure was exactly this axe violation), confirming both the violation cleared and `crossHighlight.spec.ts`'s existing assertions held. **G8 now genuinely fixed, not just plausibly fixed.** G10's timeout is a separate, unrelated finding: re-ran `just bench-docker-cold` alone (nothing else running) — clean PASS, **27.097s**, faster than the first isolated pass, not slower. Four attempts today: fail (unexplained) → clean pass, isolated (67.3s) → timeout, immediately after G8's e2e suite → clean pass, isolated (27.1s). Both isolated runs passed clean; the one failure with a plausible cause directly followed a heavy concurrent process. Reasonably good evidence the harness/product are sound and G10 is contention-sensitive rather than inherently flaky — not proof (the first failure was never root-caused). Practical takeaway: don't run G10 concurrently with `npm run test:e2e` or other heavy processes. **Addendum (2026-09-08, root cause confirmed): the 200s timeout's aftermath, not a new problem.** A subsequent `just acceptance` run (9/10 gates PASS — G1-G9 all real, including G3/G5/G8) hit G10 exit 2 with a concrete, diagnosable Docker error: `Network agentdx-g10_default ... already exists` / `Container agentdx-g10-seed-1 ... already in use by container 553f87372bc3...`. This is Finding #1 from `op2-audit-docker-cold-start.md`, live: the 200s-timeout run was killed by `just acceptance`'s own outer wrapper mid-`docker compose up -d --build`; since `-d` detaches, its containers kept running orphaned from the process tree that would have torn them down, and `_go_cold`'s `docker compose down` (exit code discarded, by design at the time) did not remove them either. Repaired same day: `_go_cold` now calls a new `_teardown_ok()` after `docker compose down` and escalates to a new `_force_teardown()` (direct `docker rm -f`/`docker network rm`) if containers or the project's default network survived, raising `CannotMeasure` with an actionable diagnostic if even that fails — the same `down`→verify→force pattern also applied to the harness's own final exit-cleanup step, to stop this from recurring for the *next* invocation. Not live-verified — no Docker daemon available to this session either; needs a real re-run. Immediate unblock for the currently-stuck state: `docker rm -f 553f87372bc3c493750a53fb0879b30c37c0cc17b8f542717925481660388f3b; docker compose -p agentdx-g10 down -v --remove-orphans; docker network rm agentdx-g10_default` (harmless if some of these no-op). **Addendum, same day: the stuck state had already self-resolved** (both cleanup commands reported nothing to remove). **A sixth attempt then produced a fourth distinct outcome:** `build_and_up_s: 215.512`, over the 180s threshold — genuinely slow, not hung or conflicted. This exposed a separate, real harness bug found while investigating: `measure()`'s poll-loop `deadline` was computed from before the build started, so a build alone exceeding the threshold meant the loop never ran even once — `healthy`/`populated` reported `False` from an unreached branch, and `detail` ("never returned 200") implied a check happened when none did. Fixed alongside: the loop now guarantees at least one real check regardless of deadline, and the failure message distinguishes "build alone blew the budget" from "health genuinely never answered." Tally across 6 attempts today: 2 clean isolated passes (67.3s, 27.1s), 4 failures each now individually diagnosed (unexplained exit-1 / outer-timeout-orphaned-container / the resulting naming conflict / this slow build) — **recommendation is to pause further G10 attempts for now**, since 6 consecutive full daemon-wide cache-wipe rebuilds in one sitting is plausible cumulative strain on Docker Desktop's VM, not necessarily a product defect; the two clean passes already prove the product itself is fast when the environment isn't under that load. **Addendum, same day: a second real bug found and fixed.** `just acceptance` hit the exact same silent 200s timeout a second time — and both times this happened specifically through `just acceptance`'s own outer wrapper, never on a standalone `just bench-docker-cold` run, which always either passed or failed with a real diagnosable error. Root cause: `tests/acceptance/test_gates.py`'s `test_g10_docker_demo_under_180s_cold` set `timeout_s=200` for the whole `just bench-docker-cold` invocation, but the inner harness's own `docker compose up -d --build` subprocess already treats `threshold_s*3` (540s) as a legitimate ceiling — and this same session measured a real 215.512s build, over a fifth of that ceiling on an ordinary run. 200s was tighter than the inner harness's own accepted worst case, not a generous backstop, and killing it mid-`up` is exactly the mechanism (orphaned detached containers) Finding #1 already traced as the cause of a later naming-conflict failure. Fixed: raised to `timeout_s=800`, comfortably past every inner sub-budget. Not yet live-verified. G3's and G5's status rows (§6) cite `op2-audit-g3-g5.md` directly for the fuller account. **Addendum (2026-09-08), a seventh attempt: another genuinely slow build, worse than the sixth.** `bench/results/docker-cold-start.json` (committed) recorded the sixth attempt's 215.512s; a later, uncommitted run on the owner's machine reached **494.43s** build+up — over 2.7x the 180s threshold, `failed_step: health` (the fixed do-while poll loop correctly identified this as "build alone blew the budget," not a health-check failure, per this row's own earlier fix). Tally now 7 attempts today: 2 clean isolated passes (67.3s, 27.1s), 5 failures (1 unexplained exit-1, 1 outer-timeout/orphaned-container since fixed, 1 resulting naming conflict since fixed, and 2 slow builds — 215.5s then 494.4s, getting worse, not better). This strengthens rather than contradicts this row's own standing theory: **7 consecutive full daemon-wide cache-wipe rebuilds in one sitting is a heavy, escalating load** on Docker Desktop's VM (each `docker builder prune -af` wipes the *entire* daemon-wide builder cache, not just this project's own layers) — a monotonically worsening build time across repeated same-day cold attempts is exactly what cumulative VM strain (disk I/O contention, memory pressure) would produce, and is not consistent with a code-level regression (no code changed between the sixth and seventh attempts). **Recommendation, now stronger than before: stop running G10 today.** Restart Docker Desktop (or take a real break) before trusting any further measurement — both of today's genuinely clean results (67.3s, 27.1s) came early, before this fatigue pattern set in, and remain the more representative numbers for what the product actually does cold. **Addendum (2026-09-08): the "VM fatigue" theory above is retracted — tested directly and found wanting.** The owner did a real, full quit-and-reopen of Docker Desktop (confirmed explicitly, not assumed) and re-ran cold: **225.003s, still over threshold.** A genuine restart not fixing it rules out simple VM fatigue as the primary cause. Two real, distinct things were actually going on, now disentangled:

1. **The slow builds (215.5s, 494.4s, 201.4s, 225.0s — four of them, none fixed by a restart) are most likely registry/network-fetch variance, not environment strain.** This file's own pre-existing module docstring (`_go_cold`, written before this session) already documented exactly this: "Two cold runs of the same commit ... measured 140.273s and 106.342s — a 34s spread explained largely by the first paying a registry pull the second did not." `docker builder prune -af` wipes the build cache but *not* pulled base images, and every cold run still re-fetches this project's 79-package `uv.lock` closure (`duckdb`/`langgraph`'s tree included) plus npm's tree fresh from the network — a real, understood source of run-to-run variance this project already knew about, not a new mystery. Not fully root-caused (no packet capture or registry-latency log exists to prove it conclusively), but it fits the evidence far better than "the VM needs a break," and nothing about it is fixable by this harness — it is inherent to what "cold" means here.

2. **A second, genuinely new and more diagnosable bug: consecutive same-day runs raced Docker's own async container removal.** Two runs back-to-back (the `just acceptance` G10 gate, then a standalone `just bench-docker-cold` moments later) both failed with `Error response from daemon: container is marked for removal and cannot be started` / `Conflict. The container name ... is already in use` — the *exact* Finding #1 symptom, despite `_teardown_ok()`/`_force_teardown()` already being in place. Root cause: `_teardown_ok()` checks `docker compose ps -aq` and `docker network inspect` at one instant, but Docker removes containers/networks *asynchronously* at the daemon level — a container can stop appearing in `ps -a` (checker says "clean") before the underlying resource is actually released, and a `docker compose up` moments later can still collide with it. One check cannot tell "gone" apart from "the daemon is still finishing." **Fixed:** a new `_teardown_settled()` (5 retries, 2s backoff) replaces the single `_teardown_ok()` check at both call sites (`_go_cold`'s pre-measurement teardown and `main`'s post-run exit cleanup) — polls instead of checking once, giving the daemon room to actually finish. Not yet live-verified (no Docker in this sandbox); this is exactly the mechanism the evidence points at, but only a real re-run proves the fix.

**Honest final tally, 10 attempts in one day:** 2 clean passes (67.3s, 27.1s, both early/isolated), 8 failures — 1 unexplained exit-1, 1 outer-timeout/orphaned-container (fixed, Finding #1), 2 async-removal-race naming conflicts (fixed just now, unverified), 4 slow-builds-over-threshold (registry/network variance, not fixed — not fixable by this harness). **The owner chose to stop chasing a green run today and commit this honest record rather than keep retrying** — consistent with this project's own standing rule that a gate's status is what was actually measured, not what a maintainer keeps trying for |

Both `uv sync` invocations now pass `--no-cache`. Deliberately left alone: `UV_COMPILE_BYTECODE=1` — also a real, if smaller, size contributor (bakes `.pyc` for every installed package), but pulling it would trade image size for slower container startup (moving Python's first-import compile cost back onto the critical path `/api/health` sits behind, i.e. directly into G10's own timing) — a functional tradeoff this session cannot test live, so kept rather than guessed at. **Not yet re-measured** — no Docker daemon in this sandbox; needs a real `docker compose -p agentdx-g10 up -d --build` + `docker images agentdx:local --format '{{.Size}}'` to confirm both that it built clean and how close to 500MB it now lands |

Two claims made when this row was first written don't hold up. First, `verdict_rules.toml`'s own comment on `fake_fanout_max_parallelism`/`fake_fanout_min_branches` ("needs a schedule-enumeration input `explore/` (P13) doesn't provide yet") is overstated: `analysis.timing.parallelism_metrics()` computes `average_parallelism` from a single already-executed run's DAG (`total_work_ms / critical_path_length_ms`) — no schedule enumeration involved, `explore/` is not a dependency here, and that TOML comment's own cross-reference ("see verdict.py's module docstring 'declared, not built' list") points at a list that doesn't exist anywhere in `verdict.py`. Second, this row's own "only a new test may be needed" is understated in the other direction: `verdict()`'s `parallelism` parameter is, per its own docstring, "accepted, not currently read" by any rule; `VerdictClass` and the recommendation-rule set have no fan-out-related member at all; and no source file anywhere in `analysis/` computes a branch/fan-out-degree count — `fake_fanout_min_branches` has no data source today, in `timing.py`, `aggregates.py`, or anywhere else (confirmed by a direct grep, not inferred). So closing row 7 needs new production code first — a branch-count computation that doesn't exist yet, plus a new recommendation rule wiring it and `average_parallelism` against the already-declared TOML thresholds — and only then the false-positive test itself. Smaller than D-86/D-87/D-88 (the PRD supplies exact threshold numbers and the row's semantics are unambiguous, unlike those three's open design questions), but not the same-day drive-by the original text here implied. Not built this pass, for the same reason: new detection logic landing on the verdict/recommendation surface is a decision for the owner, not a unilateral build, even a well-specified one. Flagged alongside D-86/D-87/D-88 rather than split off |

---

## 10. Open questions and known PRD conflicts

**Open questions.** `Decide by` is set by this ledger, not by the PRD (PRD §43.2 gives defaults without deadlines). Working to the recommended default is always permitted; changing it needs an ADR.

| ID | Question | Recommended default (PRD §43) | Decide by | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| Q-43.2.2 | SQLite → DuckDB threshold | 20 000 events, tune from benchmarks | Week 5 | OPEN — the default is implemented and configurable via `[store] duckdb_threshold_events`; no literal exists anywhere in `store/`, asserted by a source scan, so week-5 tuning is a one-line change |
| Q-43.2.3 | Calibration defaults with no profile | LLM 800 ms, tool 200 ms, agent_step 50 ms | Week 2 | OPEN — **sharper finding, 2026-09-04**: the defaults exist and are configurable (`config.py`'s `calibration_llm_ms`/`calibration_tool_ms`/`calibration_agent_step_ms`, `CalibrationProfile.defaults_only`), but `runtime/scheduler.py` has no call site for `CalibrationProfile.duration_for` at all — found while building `compare --baseline`/`analyze --scorecard` (§13): every span's virtual duration is `0` for any run through the real `Scheduler` today, so `code_pipeline`'s scorecard prints `achieved_speedup 0.00x` (honest — `compare()`'s own `t_multi_ms > 0` guard, not fabricated — but not yet a number with real magnitude either). This predates and is independent of `--baseline`/`--scorecard`'s own build; wiring calibration into the live scheduler is a `runtime/` change, not attempted here |

| Q-43.2.5 | Waterfall SVG → Canvas switchover | 20 000 spans | Week 8 | OPEN — P15 built the SVG (visx) path only, per this default; NFR-3's 5 000-span floor is well under the 20 000-span switchover point named here, so P15 did not need to resolve this question, only stay under it (confirmed via a real Playwright perf trace against a synthetic 5 280-span fixture — see Q-P15.1 below and §13's P15 entry) |
| Q-43.2.6 | Verdict weights in `verdict_rules.toml` | §18.2 defaults, tuned against fixtures, version any change | Week 6 | OPEN |
